OIL FUTURES: Prices rise as Hormuz disruption limits supply
Trump secures Russian diesel supply deal
Hormuz traffic drops to record low levels
Dated Brent premium surges above futures
Crude oil futures settled higher Oct. 9, bolstered by a physically tight market amid relentless global demand as flows through the Strait of Hormuz remained largely disrupted, though recent policy initiatives could begin to provide relief.
NYMEX November WTI settled 36 cents/barrel higher at $91.85/barrel, and ICE November Brent rose 44 cents/b to $104.72/b.
NYMEX October RBOB climbed 2.08 cents/gallon to $3.2952/ gal, while October ULSD settled 14.45 cents/gal higher at $4.7384/gal.
US President Donald Trump said via TruthSocial on Oct. 9 that he had concluded a "highly successful discussion" with Russian President Vladimir Putin, under which Russia would immediately supply over 300,000 metric tons of diesel to the "American and Global Marketplace," followed by a further 500,000 mt in November, and 1 million mt "immediately thereafter."
Trump said Russia would subsequently deliver an additional 3 million mt of diesel "within a short period of time," bringing the total commitment to around 4.8 million mt. "Between our TOTAL CONTROL of the Strait of Hormuz, and this great announcement on Russian Energy, Diesel Prices for Americans and, indeed, the World, will be COMING DOWN, IN RECORD NUMBERS, AND FAST!" Trump said.

International Crude
Qatari condensate cargo shipped to NE Asia for first time since early March: CAS
Qatar ships 2 mil barrels to South Korea in Sep
Condensate transits Hormuz via STS transfer
Exports to Jebel Ali drop sharply in 2026
One VLCC of Qatari condensate discharged at South Korea in late September, marking the first shipment of the product from Qatar to Northeast Asia since early in the US-Iran war, data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea showed.
The DHT Tiger loaded close to 2 million barrels of deodorized field condensate and low sulfur condensate Sept. 6 from Fujairah, which was eventually discharged at South Korea Sept. 25, CAS data showed.
Low sulfur condensate, which has an API gravity of 60.9 and sulfur content of 0.187%, and deodorized field condensate with an API gravity of 59.6 and sulfur content of 0.241% are the two condensates produced by state-owned QatarEnergy.
The volume onboard DHT Tiger was transferred Sept. 6 at Fujairah via ship-to-ship operations from the VLCC Kiku, which had loaded around 1 million barrels of each grade from Ras Laffan over Aug. 23-26, CAS data showed.
Prior to this, around 1 million barrels of Qatari deodorized field condensate transited the Strait of Hormuz onboard the VLCC VL Breeze, which co-loaded the condensate March 2 and finally arrived at South Korea July 19, CAS data showed.
Since March, Qatar's condensate exports have been heading primarily to ENOC's condensate refinery at Jebel Ali, with monthly volumes seen ranging from 1 million barrels to 2 million barrels, CAS data showed.
The exceptions were in June and September, when exports to Jebel Ali were at 500,250 barrels and 800,400 barrels, respectively, the data showed.
Volumes from Qatar to Jebel Ali averaged 2.21 million barrels each month in 2025, with total monthly exports of both lowsulfur condensate and deodorized field condensate averaging 5.48 million barrels through the same time period, according to CAS data.
Seven Singapore-based trade sources told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, that they expect Qatari condensate to continue moving out to Northeast Asia for a second straight month in October, with their valuations of low-sulfur condensate at

QatarEnergy and ENOC did not respond to Platts' Oct. 5 requests for official comment.
In the first week of October, the average transit count through the Strait of Hormuz was 22 per day, compared with 27 ships per day in the final week of September, CAS data showed. However, transit levels were slightly higher than the first week of September, when transits averaged around 19 vessels each day, Platts reported earlier.
The Platts-assessed second-month naphtha and gasoline swap cracks against Dubai crude swaps averaged minus $7.09/b and $20.03/b, respectively, as of the Asian close Oct. 8, compared with September averages of minus $1.97/b and $23.04/b.

PetroChina's Guangdong Petrochemical tops 70 million mt crude throughput ahead of first turnaround
Sustains CDU utilization above 105%
First turnaround scheduled for November
China National Petroleum Corp.'s Guangdong Petrochemical has processed more than 70 million metric tons of crude since its start-up, reaching the milestone at 0400 Beijing time Sept. 18, as the refinery ran well above the nameplate capacity ahead of its first turnaround, the company said on its official WeChat account late Oct. 8.
The refinery's two crude distillation units, each of 10 million metric tons/year, have maintained operating rates above 105% for an extended period. The No. 2 CDU has been processing at 1,307 mt/hour, equivalent to a utilization rate of 109.8%, with what was previously regarded as a technical "red line" now becoming a stable operating norm, the company said.
Guangdong Petrochemical's cumulative crude throughput surpassed 50 million mt on Sept. 17, 2025, reaching 50.12 million mt since the start-up, according to the company.
The refinery processed 20.06 million mt of crude oil in 2025, reflecting sustained high utilization at the complex, Guangdong Petrochemical said.
First maintenance due Guangdong Petrochemical has been preparing for its first
turnaround after the start-up. The maintenance program is scheduled to begin in late November and last until January 2027, which could temporarily reduce crude runs and refined products output.
Guangdong Petrochemical, CNPC's largest-ever single refining and petrochemicals investment project, was fully commissioned on Feb. 27, 2023. The complex comprises 20 million mt/year of refining capacity, 2.6 million mt/year of aromatics production

and 1.2 million mt/year of ethylene capacity, according to the company.
The project includes a 300,000 deadweight tons crude oil terminal and a 100,000 dwt products terminal, covering 953 hectares and 41 processing units.
Designed to process ultra-heavy and lower-quality crudes characterized by high density, sulfur, nitrogen, carbon residue, metal and acid content, the refinery has established a deepconversion processing route that maximizes value through flexible production of transportation fuels, aromatics and olefins, depending on market economics, according to the company.
-- Daisy Xu - Oil Market Specialist

CRUDE MOC: Middle East sour crude complex sees one convergence
13 Dubai partials trade during MOC One Dec-loading Murban crude cargo declared SK Energy offers Dec Murban crude

The Middle East sour crude complex saw one convergence during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 9, while selling interest for December Murban crude was seen.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai, Oman and Murban all at a premium of $9.94/b to samemonth Dubai futures at the market close, down 78 cents/b day over day.
During the MOC, 13 December Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded. The sellers were Unipec, Glencore, Mercuria and Chevron, while the buyers were Trafigura and ExxonMobil.
Unipec declared a cargo of December Murban crude to Trafigura following the convergence of 20 partials in Platts cash Dubai.
A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between two counterparties in the same direction, resulting in a full 500,000-barrel physical cargo being declared from the seller to the buyer.
South Korea's SK Energy offered a 500,000 barrel cargo of Murban crude for loading over Dec. 1-28 during the MOC, with the offer reaching a 45 cents/b premium to the grade's December official selling price, FOB Fujairah, before it was withdrawn at the 4.09 pm Singapore time (0809 GMT) mark.

Americas Crude
Venezuela's oil flows hold steady as heavy crude prices continue to weaken
Merey 16 discount to WTI widens to $9.59/b in September
Joint ventures underpin stable crude production
Canadian heavy supplies intensify competition in US market
Venezuela's crude production and exports remained stable in August, but prices for its flagship Merey 16 grade continued to weaken relative to WTI as competition from alternative heavy crude supplies in the US market intensified.
Merey 16 traded at a discount of $9.59/b to WTI in September, widening from $9.06/b in August and $9.22/b in July, according to Platts pricing data.
The weakness reflects an increasingly competitive US heavy crude market, where Gulf Coast refiners have access to alternative supplies, particularly from Canada and Mexico.
Canadian heavy crude has become an increasingly important competitor, giving refiners additional purchasing options and placing pressure on Venezuelan pricing.
Stable production, exports Venezuelan crude production stood at roughly 1.22 million
b/d in August, supported by joint ventures involving state-owned PDVSA and private partners, particularly in the Orinoco Belt.
Production from PDVSA's Orinoco Belt joint ventures reached

515,500 b/d in August, up from 510,400 b/d in July and 448,000 b/d in December 2025, according to production data reviewed by Platts.
The figures highlight the growing contribution of joint ventures to Venezuelan output, while production from PDVSA's directly operated assets has shown comparatively limited growth.
Crude exports totaled about 32 million barrels in August, with the US and India continuing to absorb the bulk of shipments.
Freight costs add pressure The weakening differential comes as sharply higher tanker
freight costs complicate the economics of moving Latin American crude to distant markets.
A US Gulf Coast-to-Asia Very Large Crude Carrier fixture currently costs around $80 million, equivalent to about $40/b, according to market participants.
At that freight rate, crude priced near $90/b would carry a delivered cost of roughly $130/b before other expenses, substantially increasing the cost for Asian buyers.
Higher insurance costs, longer voyages and the use of tankers for short shuttle runs around the Gulf have reduced vessel availability for long-haul trade.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Caribbeanto-China 130,000 mt dirty tanker at an all-time high of $300/mt on Oct. 7.
Merey's heavy characteristics also require blending with lighter hydrocarbons, or diluents, to facilitate transportation and meet commercial specifications, adding to its production and

A total of 1.459 million barrels/day of oil-equivalent output-- about 72% of total Gulf of Mexico production--was shut in as operators prepared for Hurricane Isaias to make landfall, the Marine Minerals Administration said Oct. 9.
Along with crude oil, nearly 59% of natural gas production, or 1.26 billion cubic feet/day, is also shut-in, the MMA said in its 2 pm ET update. The latest curtailed volumes compare with 1.283 million boe/d and 1.13 Bcf/d as of Oct. 8.

Hurricane Isaias threatens the northern USGC as ports tighten operations
Storm packs 121 mph winds, targets Mobile-area plants
Coast Guard sets Zulu status for Mobile port
Diesel prices surge 6% on export disruptions
Hurricane Isaias is now a Category 3 storm, approaching the northern US Gulf Coast Oct. 9 amid forecasters warning mariners to complete storm avoidance plans immediately as the system neared landfall, threatening refinery, terminal, and shipping operations from Mississippi to the Florida Panhandle, according to the National Hurricane Center.
Forecasters said Isaias is expected to approach the northern Gulf Coast later Oct. 9, making landfall within the hurricane warning area that evening. The hurricane was packing maximum sustained winds of about 121 miles per hour with stronger gusts, maintaining Category 3 strength on the Saffir-Simpson Hurricane Wind Scale.
A Category 3 hurricane carries sustained winds of 96112 knots and is capable of causing devastating damage,

including widespread power outages, significant structural impacts, and dangerous coastal and marine conditions. The expected evening landfall could disrupt vessel traffic, port operations, and energy infrastructure across portions of the northern USGC.
At 10 am CT, the center was located about 195 miles south of Pensacola, Florida. The storm was moving north-northeast at 16 mph, a motion expected to continue through the evening of Oct. 9 before turning more directly north Oct. 10.
The storm's track and intensity continue to pose significant risks to USGC shipping and offshore operations. Maritime authorities urged mariners to remain in an avoidance posture or to urgently execute hurricane plans, as conditions are expected to deteriorate rapidly along the coast ahead of landfall.
The NHC hurricane warning area was extended from Ocean Springs, Mississippi, eastward to the Bay-Gulf County line in the Florida Panhandle, including coastal Alabama and the Mobile and Pensacola regions.
The US Coast Guard placed Mobile under Port Condition Zulu Oct. 9, indicating that sustained gale-force winds of 3954 mph were expected within 12 hours. The Mississippi River update represented the most significant Coast Guard notice for regional refinery, terminal, and shipping operations Friday morning.
Of the USGC refineries in the Mobile region, Chevron Corp.'s Pascagoula, Mississippi, refinery is most likely to be impacted, with the possibility of power snags once the storm makes landfall, and Vertex Energy's smaller Mobile, Alabama, plant may also feel some impact.
Chevron said in an Oct. 8 statement that it continues to follow its storm preparedness procedures and is closely monitoring the hurricane's projected path. A spokesperson for Vertex was not immediately available for comment.
Farther west, in New Orleans, the Coast Guard maintained modified Port Condition X-ray on the Mississippi River between Mile 93 above Head of Passes and Mile 20 below Head of Passes because of the storm, although it was not anticipating a waterway closure. Southwest Pass bar pilot operations were suspended because of offshore conditions.
A major swathe of the US Gulf Coast refinery fleet is likely to emerge unscathed by the storm, with just about 500,000 barrels/ day, or about 2.5%, of total US refining capacity at possible risk in the Mobile area.
But tight global diesel markets have made the USGC the premier supplier of the fuel, and a shipping disruption is likely to affect export flows to Latin America and Europe.
US Gulf Coast ultra low sulfur diesel (ULSD) prices rose sharply Oct. 8. Platts assessed exports at $4.5414/gallon, and the pipeline price at $4.8644/gal, both up about 6%. The pipeline market's discount to NYMEX futures narrowed 47% to 1.85 cents/ gal from 3.50 cents/gal. US Gulf Coast waterborne ULSD rose just under 6% to $4.9819/gal.
-- Janet Mcgurty

ARA jet fuel stocks rise 9.61% week over week: Insights Global
Net oil stocks drop 2.21% in ARA hub
Jet-diesel spread widens to $15.30/b premium
Jet fuel stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub rebounded 9.61% week over week to 479,000 metric tons as the jet-diesel regrade strengthened, reversing the previous week's draw but remaining critically low amid ongoing tightness in the European market, according to the Oct. 8 data from Insights Global.
Net oil stocks fell to 4.012 million mt, a 2.21% draw, reflecting the wider supply situation within oil product markets, the data showed. Spot prices remain near all-time highs as Russian and Chinese export bans caused increased competition for the already limited barrels coming into Europe. China export ban has ended as of Oct. 8 following the national week holiday, which could ease supply tightness in European markets.
The build in jet fuel comes as the jet-diesel regrade strengthened from the week prior, making jet fuel production more attractive compared with diesel. The jet fuel crack spread against dated Brent Oct. 8 was at $97.18/barrel, putting it at a $5.79 premium to ULSD, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
The jet vs diesel barge regrade was even higher, at $15.30/b Oct. 8, down from a five-month high the previous day, Platts data showed.
"Jet is stronger than diesel now because refineries were max diesel for a while; volumes from the US have dipped and Chinese export restrictions mean East of Suez exports are also not increasing," a Europe-based distillates trader said, adding that diesel has seen some alleviation of tightness from strategic stock releases. "Now [the market] is pricing for that switch back to jet for refineries," another trader added.
Europe's imports of jet fuel from the US hit 42,000 mt in October so far, down from 248,000 mt last month, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. About 731,000 mt of jet fuel was exported from the US to Europe in August. South Korea has exported 491,000 mt of jet fuel to Europe so far in October, down from the record highs of 571,000 mt in September, CAS data showed, as the country remained a key supplier while imports from the US remained low.
The outlook remains mixed as low inventories, seasonal

refinery maintenance and uncertain import flows could keep jet fuel differentials supported, even as market sources report relatively strong demand compared with 2025.
-- Oskar Leskow, Aruni Sunil
Europe jet fuel/kerosene imports reach 1.3 mil mt so far in October
India, South Korea, Nigeria major jet fuel/kerosene suppliers
UK European jet fuel intake at 288,000 mt in Oct. so far
Europe's jet fuel and kerosene imports reached 1.3 million metric tons between Oct. 1-8, so far lower than the total of 2 million mt imported during all of September, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data.
European jet fuel/kerosene imports in October so far include 385,000 mt from India, higher than the 197,000 mt imported in September and 491,000 mt from South Korea, so far lower compared to the 571,000 mt discharged last month, CAS data showed.
Meanwhile, the US has discharged 42,000 mt of jet fuel and kerosene into Europe in October so far, whereas in September it supplied a total of 248,000 mt, data showed. Nigeria has discharged 133,000 mt so far in October, compared with 386,000 mt in all of September, according to CAS data.
The UK imported 288,000 mt of jet fuel between Oct. 1-8, compared with a total of 691,000 mt in September, data showed.
Spain has taken in 283,000 mt so far in October, up from 177,000 mt last month, while the Netherlands imported 442,000 mt, up from 336,000 mt in September, according to CAS data.
The European jet fuel market is expected to tighten over the next few weeks as flows from Asia decline due to Chinese export restrictions, those from the US drop due to low stocks and local refineries have prioritized diesel production over the last few months.
Refineries have been prioritizing diesel output in the wake of the Russian export ban.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the regrade, the spread between the European jet barge crack and diesel barge crack at minus $11.94/barrel on Aug. 21. A negative regrade incentivizes refineries to produce more diesel fuel over its codistillate jet.
Now, after two months of refiners prioritizing diesel output, the jet fuel market has tightened, resulting in the regrade flipping to positive.
The regrade hit a five-month high of $16.45/b on Oct. 7, the highest level since April 28.
"Jet is stronger than diesel now because refineries were max diesel for a while, volumes from the US have dipped and Chinese export restrictions mean East of Suez exports are also not increasing," said an European distillates trader, adding that diesel has seen some alleviation of tightness from strategic stock releases.

ARA diesel, gasoil stocks fall 2% week over week: Insights Global

European diesel prices surge $67/mt to $1,496.50

Diesel and gasoil stocks in the Amsterdam-RotterdamAntwerp refining hub fell 40,000 metric tons, or 2.20%, to 1.776 million mt in the week ended October 8, the latest data from Insights Global showed.
Inventories remain 21.10% lower than the volumes seen in the same week of 2025.
The withdrawal comes against a backdrop of constrained supply in Europe, despite the recent G7 commitment to release up to 100 million barrels of oil over the next four months.
The announcement of the stock releases eased market sentiment and sent European diesel prices plummeting on Oct. 2. They have since rallied amid geopolitical headlines, with no real change to tight fundamentals, say market participants.
Platts assessed the 10ppm CIF NWE diesel flat price up $67/ mt to $1,496.50/mt on Oct. 9.
The price is moving with, "Trump's tweets and developments in the US- Iran war" said one European diesel trader.
They also said that "hurricane risk in the USGC could affect flows coming to NWE and then to the Med."
Europe's diesel/gasoil imports are expected to reach 3.5 million metric tons in October, slightly higher than the 3.4 million mt in September but lower than the 4.4 million mt imported in October 2025, according to Kpler.
Amid weaker inflows, diesel margins in Europe have fallen relative to jet fuel, potentially reducing domestic output and increasing reliance on reserves to supply the market.
The Rotterdam ULSD crack spread versus Dated Brent

stood at $63.05/b on Oct. 8, below the average of $81.73/b for September, Platts data showed.
Low Rhine levels impact A European-based market participant said recent rainfall had
brought only limited relief to the Rhine, with additional water levels proving insufficient to meaningfully improve navigability conditions. The source noted forecasts for a dry October, suggesting little rainfall for the remainder of the month, warning that river systems were unlikely to recover materially. According to the source, the market continues to operate in survival mode amid persistent logistical constraints and uncertainty over future water levels.
They added that barge movements remained heavily restricted. The source described such volumes as "close to nothing", highlighting the limited impact current barge movements are having on regional supply chains.
In the late afternoon hours of Oct. 9, Rhine water levels rose to around 9 cm at the critical chokepoint of Kaub, according to data from Germany's Directorate-General for Waterways and Shipping. Water levels are expected to hover around the same levels over the next 24 hours before rising in the days that follow according to the forecast.
The same source added that while seasonal heating demand had strengthened somewhat, elevated product prices were discouraging consumers from purchasing larger volumes.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 50ppm gasoil FOB ARA barge flat price Oct. 8, $77 higher at $1,467/mt, and at $45/mt discount to the front-month ICE LSGO futures contract.
50ppm gasoil is a fuel grade used as heating oil in Germany and Switzerland.
For comparison, the flat price of 50 ppm gasoil barges remains above $745.75/mt seen on Feb. 27, the day before the Middle East war started.

European diesel/gasoil imports expected to reach 3.5 mil mt in October
India, US to supply over 1 mil mt each
European markets struggle with high diesel prices
Europe's diesel/gasoil imports are expected to reach 3.5 million metric tons in October, slightly higher than the 3.4 million mt in September but lower than the 4.4 million mt imported in the same period in 2025, according to Kpler.
Of the total, around 1.2 million mt is expected to come from the US, lower than the 1.8 million mt that arrived in September and the 1.4 million mt in the same month last year. All US imports are expected to come from PADD 3 (US Gulf Coast), with none expected from PADD 1 (Atlantic Coast), Kpler data showed.
Meanwhile, 1.1 million mt is expected to come from India, the highest since October 2025 and nearly double the 573,000 mt in September, data showed.
Additionally, 176,000 mt is expected to be supplied from Saudi Arabia, down from 396,000 mt in September.
The sustained high price environment in Europe is diminishing buying interest for diesel at the wholesale and retail level, according to market participants.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the 10 parts per million diesel CIF NWE cargo price at an all-time high of $1,664.75/ mt on Sept. 15. Since then, the high cost of diesel has trickled down to the pump, sending retail prices surging and hampering end consumption.
In the week ended Oct. 5, UK diesel pump prices rose to their highest recorded level at 119.52 pence/liter, according to the latest data from the UK Department of Energy Security and Net Zero. This marks an increase of 56.6 pence/liter from the same week last year.
The rest of Europe is experiencing similar peaks, with pump prices averaging about 2.15/liter across all EU countries in the week ended Oct. 5, down 9 cents/l from the all-time high of 2.24/liter the week prior, according to data from the European Commission published Oct. 8.
"Consumer behavior is changing [with high retail prices]," said an European diesel trader. "People are filling up less, taking fewer journeys or looking for alternative transport. This is happening across Europe."

While particularly exacerbated by record high flat prices, weaker demand for road fuels has been a consistent theme since the start of the US-Iran war amid reduced energy security.
Meanwhile, around 968,000 mt of diesel/gasoil were loaded in the week ended Oct. 4, up from the 922,000 mt loaded the previous week, according to Kpler.
-- David Neef, Sophia Aung
US middle distillates exports to Europe to reach 1.5 mil mt in Oct: Kpler

G7 stock release shifts regional economics
US middle distillates exports to Europe are expected to reach around 1.5 million metric tons in October, down from 2 million in September, Kpler data showed.
US diesel and gasoil imports to Europe are expected to reach around 1.2 million mt in October, down compared with 1.8 million mt in September and down from the 1.4 million mt that arrived in October 2025. All volumes are expected to come from PADD 3 (US Gulf Coast), with none expected to come from PADD 1 (Atlantic Coast), according to Kpler.
However, recent price volatility and fluctuating arbitrage economics are creating uncertainty about the viability of future flows from the US to Europe.
The transatlantic diesel arbitrage closed on Oct. 2, as ICE low sulfur gasoil futures plummeted following the commitment from the G7 to release up to 100 million barrels of oil stocks in the coming four months.
"The HO-GO spread moved up quite significantly with the slide on gasoil and the TA arbitrage is now closed on a prompt basis," one Europe-based diesel trader said.
On Oct. 7, the US Gulf Coast-Northwest Europe and USGCMediterranean stood firmly in negative territory at minus $2.19/b and minus $4.50/b, respectively
A second European diesel trader said, "When you have an announcement, such as the G7 stock release, you change the economics of multiple regions."
"Some vessels are arriving across the region because it was economical two weeks ago, but now some vessels may do a U-turn from the US," they added.
They further said that "volatility is unlinked with reality," and that this is causing an "increase in the frequency of arbitrage openings and closing cycles."

Singapore's onshore commercial middle distillate stocks rose 6.03% week over week to 9.37 million barrels over Oct. 1-7, data from Enterprise Singapore showed Oct. 8.
Stocks hit a 21-week high and were last recorded higher at 10.36 million barrels in the week ended May 13, historical data showed.
The city-state's gasoil imports fell 70.03% week over week to 40,879 metric tons, a six-week low. Imports were previously lower in the week of Aug. 20-26 at 3,176 mt, according to the data.
Imports from China rose 23.50% week over week to 40,879 mt, making it the top supplier. Malaysia supplied 85.14% less volumes over the week, at 4,438 mt. Notably, rare inflows were seen from Australia, typically an importer of gasoil, at 5,023 mt.
Singapore's gasoil exports declined 21.32% week over week to 290,413 mt, Enterprise data showed.
Australia remained the top recipient despite volumes falling 55.42% over the week to 76,482 mt.
Cambodia and Papua New Guinea also received fewer volumes at 13,495 mt and 5,179 mt respectively, compared with 22,668 mt and 27,015 mt in the previous week.
Malaysia received 55,378 mt of gasoil, followed by Indonesia at 54,583 mt and South Africa at 29,200 mt.
Singapore exported 28,218 mt of gasoil to Myanmar, 20,971 mt to the Philippines, 5,918 mt to Fiji, and 984 mt to New Caledonia, the data showed.
Jet fuel imports up, exports fall Singapore's jet fuel imports rose 48.43% to 41,995 mt over Oct.
1-7, up from 28,293 mt the previous week, Enterprise Singapore data showed.
Inflows from China of 24,082 mt, India of 10,652 mt, and Malaysia of 7,226 mt, predominantly supported jet fuel imports, according to the data.
Meanwhile, imports from Japan fell from an over-four-year high the previous week to nearly none at 0.002 mt in the week ended Oct. 7, the data showed.
The city-state's jet fuel/kerosene exports declined 80.65% from a week earlier to 13,478 mt, according to the data.
South Africa and New Caledonia were the largest destinations, absorbing 12,156 mt and 792 mt, respectively, the data showed.
The remaining outflows were led by Fiji with 495 mt and Malaysia with 33 mt, according to the data, with smaller cargoes trickling to India, the Philippines, Thailand, Australia, the UK, and Sri Lanka.

Marine Fuel
BUNKERWORLD INDEX: Prices up for sixth week straight as supply concerns persist
Marine fuel costs surge 5% week on week
Middle East supply tightens amid attacks
Singapore fuel stocks hit two-month low
Bunker prices continued their ascent in the week to Oct. 8, as supply in the Middle East and Black Sea, combined with surging freight rates, drove prices up at more than twice the rate of crude.
The Platts Bunkerworld Marine Fuel 0.5% Sulfur Index ended the week on Oct. 8 at $882/mt, touching the highest levels since May after climbing $19/mt week on week. The Platts BW380 Index, which represents value for 3.5% sulfur fuel oil, also approached a four-month high, rising for a sixth consecutive week to $765/mt.
Over the week, the high sulfur fuel oil index climbed by 5%, compared to a 2% increase in the December Brent crude futures contract across the seven-day period.
Crude futures strengthened significantly between Oct. 7 and Oct. 8 after reports of a new attack on a chemicals tanker off Qatar and close to 1.3 million b/d of production shut-ins on the US Gulf Coast due to storm conditions. Meanwhile, extreme freight rates have continued to distort conventional market dynamics.
"VLCCs are stable at around $80 million, which is the equivalent of a mind-blowing $40/b to move oil," said Scott Shelton, energy specialist at the data provider TP ICAP. "I see a lot of failed fixings out of PADD 3, with only one VLCC booked to Japan for Nov. 19 at $81 million," he said.
As fuel costs have skyrocketed, uncertainty has prevailed around the future of a proposed global carbon tax, which was due to be voted on by the IMO in 2025 but in October that year the vote was delayed.
With members of the international organization due to meet later this year, John Denholm, chairman of the International Chamber of Shipping suggested a tradeable compliance scheme could help overcome the political deadlock on the levy. "A tradable quota would have exactly the same impact as a cash fund," he said, noting lasting US opposition to the tax.
Traffic reroutes In regional markets, conflict-driven trade disruptions have
continued to impact bunkering activity, with shipowners avoiding hotspots for targeted tanker attacks and stocks still volatile.

Resid
Black Sea strikes weigh on Istanbul bunker demand
STS bunkering calls at Istanbul fall 21% YOY as shipping activity drops: CAS
Bunker activity down at Black Sea ports; Novorossiysk calls down 92% from Q1 to Q3
Joint War Committee designates Black Sea as high risk for maritime insurers
Bunker fuel demand in Istanbul has weakened as escalating attacks on commercial vessels in the Black Sea discouraged shipping activity through the region Oct. 9, according to market participants active in the port.
The slowdown has reduced the number of vessels seeking fuel in Istanbul, a key bunkering hub for ships transiting between the Mediterranean and the Black Sea. Market sources said demand had faltered recently as shipowners and operators reassessed voyages amid heightened security risks.
Ship-to-Ship bunkering calls at Istanbul fell to 790 in September 2026, a 21% year-over-year decline and the weakest monthly reading of the year, according to data from S&P Global Commodities at Sea. Market sources said the drop reflected a broader pullback in vessel traffic through the port, itself a consequence of surging maritime risk in the Black Sea.
The decline was also evident at Black Sea ports. STS bunkering calls in Novorossiysk, Russia, fell 92% between the first and third quarters of 2026, according to CAS data. The hub recorded no STS bunkering calls in September 2026, compared with 46 in September 2025.
In Constanta, Romania, STS bunkering calls declined by 18% month over month between August and September 2026.
The threat to shipping rose sharply in the third quarter of 2026. Maritime risk consultancy Windward reported that roughly 86 ships were attacked during the period, compared with 23 in the first half of the year. The consultancy added that vessels trading with either Russia or Ukraine faced heightened exposure.
"Russia and Ukraine are now attacking any ship that trades with the other side," Windward said in a report. "In late 2025, Ukraine's attacks were limited to three vessels, all stateless shadow-fleet tankers. By Q3, it was hitting any ship calling at Russian ports."
Windward added that "Russia concentrated on ships at Greater Odesa and Reni, carrying out 10 of 16 attacks in September."
The surge in incidents prompted an escalation in the insurance community's risk assessment. On Sept. 16, the Joint War Committee -- the body that designates areas insurers treat as high risk -- added the Black Sea to its Listed Areas publication. The listing covers open waters but excludes the territorial waters of adjoining countries other than Russia and Ukraine, meaning vessels transiting the broader basin face substantially higher war risk insurance premiums, adding to the financial deterrent for

ARA fuel oil inventories fall to 918,000 mt

Bunker demand stays low amid price volatility
Fuel oil stocks in the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp refining hub fell 4.5% week over week to 918,000 mt in the seven days to Oct. 8, Insights Global data showed, after building the previous week.
Fuel oil's share of overall oil product inventories in the ARA region climbed to 22%.
In the Northwest European high sulfur fuel oil market, traders continued to report limited demand.
Traders attributed the weak appetite to volatility in Brent futures and fluctuating ZRE-A ticket prices in Rotterdam.
On the supply side, participants reported no issues, although some raised concerns about the prompt availability of on-spec RMG. This followed the arrival of volumes from Latin America in September.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month HSFO crack spread at minus $8.05/b, up $3.55/b from minus $11.60/b the previous week.
Market participants have described the Northwest European VLSFO complex as well offered, while local bunkering demand has been subdued amid higher flat prices.
"There has been some volume fixed" on arbitrage opportunities out of Europe, which has provided some support to the complex, one trader said.
However, dirty tanker freight rates remain extremely elevated, which could pressure further volumes to move out of Europe.
Meanwhile, market participants pointed to increased blending demand in ARA for 1% product.
"1% is strong again," said another trader said, noting "double digit premiums" in the region.
In the paper marker, the front-month EU fuel oil 0.5%S marine fuel oil structure was assessed at a backwardation of $16.50/mt on Oct. 8, up $3.75/mt week over week.
The 3.5%S fuel oil FOB Rotterdam barge front-month time spread equivalent was assessed at a backwardation of $25.25/ mt, widening by $9/mt.

Bunker market In the Northwest European bunker market, ARA continued to
see low demand as volatility in the futures market kept buyers cautious.
Falling paper values early in the week led buyers to wait for further declines, and rising flat prices later kept buying cautious.
In Rotterdam and Amsterdam, several suppliers could not offer prompt VLSFO, and delivery dates for available volumes moved into the week starting Oct. 11.
Market participants said logistical constraints were lingering following blending issues that raised quality concerns in late September.
HSFO availability improved from the end of September, though views were mixed as some suppliers reported prompt tightness.
By comparison, marine gasoil was comfortably supplied, with prompt deliveries available across the region.
In the German port of Hamburg, HSFO remained tight, although low demand helped ease pressure.
Further north, Skaw was more balanced, with suppliers at the Danish hub offering all grades with no availability issues reported.

Feedstocks
Asian naphtha cracks near three-month highs as freight costs rise on Middle East tensions
High freight erodes arbitrage economics
Gulf to north Asia freight at record high
Asian naphtha cracks climbed to near three-month highs as elevated freight costs limit arbitrage economics and heightened Middle East risks fuel supply concerns, according to data from Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, and market participants.
The CFR Japan naphtha physical crack spread against frontmonth ICE Brent crude futures rose to $190.18/metric ton at the Oct. 8 Asia close, up $13.70/mt from the previous session. The crack was last higher at $191.70/mt July 29, Platts data showed.
Higher freight rates have diminished arbitrage incentives to move cargoes for November delivery from the West of Suez into

Asia, market participants said. According to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, 715,092
metric tons of US Gulf Coast naphtha, 499,644 mt from North Africa and 140,613 mt from the Mediterranean are expected to be discharged in the Far East during October. Shipments from the Middle East are expected to still be the largest by region in October, at about 1.27 million mt, the CAS data showed.
The East-West spread will have to stay elevated to incentivize cargoes to move East, a Singapore-based trader said.
Brokers pegged the front-month East-West spread at $76.75/ mt in the afternoon of Oct. 9, $4.75/mt higher than $72/mt assessed at the Oct. 8 Asia close. The spread was last wider at $74.75/mt July 23, Platts data showed.
A rise in ship attacks has disrupted traffic through the Strait of Hormuz, a key route for Asian naphtha exports from the Middle East.
The latest incident occurred Oct. 7, when the Antigua and Barbuda-flagged oil and chemicals tanker Acers was struck by multiple projectiles off Qatar's coast, maritime risk management company Vanguard Tech said Oct. 8.
The incident marked the first attack in weeks to occur deep inside Gulf waters, with most recent attacks concentrated around the Strait of Hormuz, the United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations confirmed.
Rising freight costs Freight to move naphtha from the Gulf to north Asia is at
a record high because several LR2 tankers have switched to the dirty segment this year in a bid to capture higher earnings. The rest of the clean tanker supply is split into a two-tier market, where only a small section of owners is ready to transit the risky waters of the Strait of Hormuz, according to participants.
Even a short transit through the strait from any port inside the Gulf to Oman or west coast India for a ship-to-ship transfer can cost an LR1 anywhere between $4 million and $6 million, depending on the additional war risk premium and whether it is included or not in the freight.
"It is very random and the AWRP can vary between just under $1 million to over $3 million, depending on the maritime insurance company's profile," said a broker in Copenhagen.
At least 90 LR2s have switched to moving dirty cargoes so far this year, tightening the supply of clean tankers, according to the estimates of brokers.
Platts assessed the benchmark LR1 Gulf-Japan freight route at a historic high of w925 Oct. 8, which translates to more than $205/mt, or almost $11.3 million for a 55,000 mt cargo, up from $2.75 million, a day before the war commenced.
Prior to the war, the freight charged was lower even for the biggest crude cargo loading on a VLCC for the US Gulf-North Asia routes, Platts data showed.

European naphtha crack hits multimonth high on gasoline strength, supply concerns
Naphtha crack hits multimonth peak at minus $2.80/b
Gasoline blending demand tightens octane supply
Hurricane threatens US Gulf Coast energy output
The European naphtha front-month crack rose to a multimonth high Oct. 9 amid soaring gasoline prices.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the naphtha frontmonth crack in Northwest Europe at minus $2.80/b on the day, the highest level since Aug. 28, when it stood at minus $2.60/b.
European naphtha values drew support from strengthening gasoline blending economics, with market sources pointing to low high-octane blendstock availability.
"[It's] picking up on blending values, light virgin naphtha is tight," a participant active in the naphtha market said.
Rising blending demand was further reflected in a sharp rebound in the Eurobob gasoline market, where forward values strengthened significantly, with low-octane components particularly constrained.
"[EBOB is] back to madness, [it] was trading [at] November plus $155 yesterday, today [it's at] plus $200," the participant said.
The strength was largely attributed to robust octane-driven demand, which continued to underpin blending values and lend support to naphtha.
Market participants also pointed to broader supply concerns, including the potential impact of hurricane activity along the US Gulf Coast, which may tighten gasoline component balances and contribute to strength across Atlantic Basin blending markets.
"It's tight everywhere, maybe [because of] a hurricane on the Gulf Coast," the participant said.
The US National Hurricane Center on Oct. 8 forecast Hurricane Isaias, currently packing 85-mile-per-hour winds, to strengthen before making landfall along the northern Gulf Coast late Oct. 9 or early Oct. 10, which would likely disrupt the region's energy infrastructure, fundamentals and prices.
In a webcast around 11 am ET on Oct. 8, NHC Director Michael Brennan said the storm would bring "multiple dangerous hazards," so the center has issued hurricane warnings for parts of the Alabama, Florida and Mississippi coastlines.
-- Barbara Fernandez-pita

The resumption of shipments to Vietnam and Indonesia contributed to the rebound in exports, although total volumes remained subdued, according to the market participants.
The Singapore bitumen market remained firm in the week to Oct. 7, with tight prompt availability supporting elevated export offers. Offers for PEN 60-70 bitumen were heard at $800-$820/ mt FOB Singapore during the week, reflecting continued supply constraints.

JAPAN DATA: Household propane gas consumption falls 1.1% YOY in Aug
Average propane prices up 8.06% YOY
Total gas spending rises 2.62%
Japan's household consumption of propane gas declined 1.07% year over year to an average of 1.76 cubic meters in August, the fourth consecutive month of year-over-year fall, and spending rose 6.9% to ¥1,378 ($8.72) over the same period, the Ministry of Internal Affairs and Communications said Oct. 9.
The average purchase price of propane gas rose 8.06% year over year to ¥782.51 in August.
Household spending on city gas declined 0.2% year over year to ¥1,992 in August, and total gas expenditure grew 2.62% to ¥3,370 over the same period, the ministry said.

Washington and Tehran have been fighting over control of the Strait of Hormuz, a chokepoint handling 20% of global seaborne oil flows, since the conflict began at the end of February. The International Maritime Organization estimates that around 100 ships have been attacked in the region, while 24 seafarers were killed in the incidents.
With many shipowners in the private sector avoiding the region due to heightened security risks and high insurance premiums, Gulf states have been using state-owned tankers to shuttle oil to the Gulf of Oman before discharging the barrels onto their buyers' ships.
"Iran will continue to exert influence over the Strait of Hormuz across the longer term, so I expect that these shuttling networks will remain in place for years, not months," BRS Shipbrokers Head of Research Andrew Wilson told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
Diplomatic efforts by Iran, Oman and the US to restore normal Hormuz traffic have yielded little concrete results, with Tehran planning to charge insurance and other fees for future passage, a scheme opposed by the West and shipping industry groups.
"Tankers are now viewed by Gulf producers as vital export infrastructure," Wilson said. "Previously, infrastructure was seen as stopping at the export terminal, but I suggest that this point has now moved to the Gulf of Oman."

Tankers
Gulf states expand tanker fleets as Hormuz risks reshape export strategy
Middle Eastern countries want to own more ships High freight rates result in short payback periods Sovereign cover could complement private insurers
Middle Eastern countries have embarked on national tanker fleet expansions following the outbreak of the US-Iran war, which industry experts believe are aimed at enhancing their longterm export capabilities amid enduring disruptions at the Strait of Hormuz.

State interests Firms controlled by Bahri, ADNOC Logistics & Services, Asyad
Shipping, and Kuwait Petroleum Corp. own 89 crude tankers with a carrying capacity of 25.8 million deadweight tons and 106 chemical/product tankers with 6.71 million dwt on the waters, according to company reports and S&P Global Energy Horizons data.
In early August, ADNOC L&S, the listed shipping arm of the UAE's Abu Dhabi National Oil Co., announced its purchases of six VLCCs and five very large gas carriers. KPC has been seeking to expand its own tanker fleet to shuttle services, according to company executives.
Asyad has an order book of 14 tankers built before the current

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

16

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 9, 2026

crisis, though the Omani state-backed company said it has been avoiding Hormuz passage for safety reasons. Iraqi Oil Tankers Co. transported crude out of Hormuz with a VLCC under its operation for the first time in early October, and the company plans to purchase and own crude tankers, according to the state-run Iraqi News Agency.
Fotios Katsoulas, tanker research director at S&P Global Energy Horizons, said fleet expansion would allow Middle Eastern oil producers to maintain exports during periods when ship availability tightens and freight rates spike.
"What we are seeing is primarily a long-term strategic shift rather than a temporary reaction to the current conflict," Katsoulas said. "Owning tanker capacity is increasingly being viewed as part of a broader energy security strategy."
Economics Increased STS transfers have led to higher tanker
requirements while risk-averse ship operators are steering away from the region, tightening tonnage supply and prompting freight rates to keep breaking records in the Middle East and beyond in recent weeks.
Platts assessed the VLCC rate for transporting 265,000 metric tons of crude from the Persian Gulf to West Coast India, another emerging STS hub, at $98.01/mt on Oct. 8, equivalent to a lumpsum cost of nearly $26 million. This was 412% higher than the pre-conflict level on Feb. 27.
A roundtrip voyage for this trade could take less than two weeks. In contrast, the secondhand price for a 15-year-old secondhand VLCC is $160 million despite rising 177% year on year, according to Xclusiv Shipbrokers' estimate. Based on prevailing spot freight rates and excluding operating, financing and insurance costs, the figures suggest a payback period of roughly three months.
"It is ... relatively easy for a producer to amortize the price of a VLCC," said Wilson, adding that buying tankers would allow the producer to keep assets for a longer term compared with periodchartering ships.

Trade-offs Aside from free-on-board sales from their home ports, Middle
Eastern producers traditionally relied more on chartering ships for some months or years rather than buying tankers outright when in need to deliver oil to their customers.
But shipowners in time charters could refuse to enter war zones, and Andrew Rigden Green, a Hong Kong-based partner of law firm WFW, said charterers could face higher payments if there are delays -- and scheduling tends to be more difficult during conflicts as seafarers spend time finding safest transit means.
"From an operational perspective, ownership mainly provides security of capacity, faster reaction time, greater control over vessel positioning and less dependence on charter-market availability," said Eirini Diamantara, an Xclusiv researcher.
"The trade-off, of course, is that the owner assumes the capital cost, operating cost and asset-value risk."
In the event of a total loss of a laden VLCC, Middle Eastern producers could face losses in the range of hundreds of million dollars on top of potential cleaning costs for oil spills without insurance coverage. But cargo insurance premiums for Hormuz transits have increased by at least 30 times during the conflict, and hull premiums by 40 times, according to insurance industry participants.
Saudi and Emirati state-backed firms have been expanding maritime insurance offerings, which could lead to more coverage availability. That would complement commercial insurers rather than squeeze them out, according to some brokers.
While there have been government offerings on hull and cargo, it would be challenging for sovereign funds to cover third-party liabilities like pollution due to the difficulties in limiting damage, according to Marcus Baker, insurance brokerage Marsh's global head for marine.
Alec Russell, managing director of marine cargo at brokerage Gallagher, separately said cargo insurance is "incredibly cost effective" as a "risk-transfer mechanism".
"Even at a massive sovereign level, there's still a big requirement," Russel added. "I don't think that will change in the long run."
-- Max Lin
CMB.TECH starts operating world's first ammonia-fueled dry bulk ship
Belgian firm launches 210,000-dwt ship
Fleet expansion targets 21 ships by 2029
Green ammonia costs much more than VLSFO
CMB.TECH's first ammonia-capable ship has embarked on its maiden voyage, the Belgian shipowner said Oct. 9 while reiterating its ambition to decarbonize fleet operations with the novel marine fuel.
The 210,000-dwt Mineral Europa, billed by the company as the world's first ammonia-powered dry bulk vessel, has departed on its inaugural journey to Australia. The vessel is equipped with a

© 2026 by S&P Global Inc. All rights reserved.

17

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 9, 2026

dual-fuel engine capable of operating on conventional fuels as well as ammonia.
"After almost a decade of hard work, we are proving that decarbonizing shipping is no longer a distant ambition," CMB. TECH CEO Alexander Saverys said in a statement.
The vessel's departure marks a significant milestone for ammonia as a marine fuel, with the world's first ammoniacapable ships for deepsea trades starting to hit the waters in recent quarters.
CMB.TECH is set to add 21 ammonia-powered ships to its fleet between now and 2029, comprising 18 dry bulk carriers, one container ship and two chemical tankers, according to the statement. Most of the vessels are due to be delivered within the next nine months.
The company has sought to position itself as an early mover in ammonia-fueled shipping, investing not only in vessels but also in the supporting supply chain. CMB.TECH said it is developing ammonia storage solutions and bunker barges as part of efforts to build a fuel ecosystem capable of supplying ocean-going vessels safely and efficiently.
The latest development comes months after CMB.TECH agreed to charter 12 ammonia-powered Newcastlemaxes to Australian mining company Fortescue, which itself has plans to become a top green ammonia producer.
To secure future fuel supplies, CMB.TECH has also entered into renewable ammonia sourcing arrangements. In December 2025, the company agreed to purchase green ammonia from China's CEEC Hydrogen Energy, which has a 158,000 metric ton per year plant in Jilin Province.
Ammonia, when generated from renewable energy, is a lowcarbon fuel that has strong potential to decarbonize shipping, but it is highly toxic and corrosive and remains much more expensive than oil-based fuels.
The Platts global bunker fuel cost calculator put delivered bunker prices for very low sulfur fuel oil in Singapore at an average of $826.10/metric ton through August, compared with green ammonia cargo delivered to Far East Asia at $1,436.59/mt of VLSFO equivalent. Platts is part of S&P Global Energy.

Platts East of Suez Dirty Tanker Daily Commentary
East of Suez VLCC market edges higher
VLCC owners increasingly consider long-haul voyages
Southeast Asia Aframax awaits firmer rate tests
The East of Suez VLCC market strengthened slightly Oct. 9, with very few cargoes seen openly seeking VLCCs.
Among fresh Red Sea cargoes, Pertamina was heard to be looking for a VLCC for loading Oct. 27 from Yanbu to Cilacap.
There was also an emerging shift in owners' positioning. As the time charter equivalent earnings gap between the Gulf of Oman and Atlantic markets narrowed, owners were becoming more willing to consider long-haul voyages, market sources said.

Assessments from market sources, however, remained broadly steady day over day.
The Suezmax market was adjusted slightly higher, in line with indications from market sources, who said owners were in a good position to look to push rates above the last done levels. Indian charterers were heard seeking vessels for regional loadings from Basrah and Ras Tanura.
In the Southeast Asian Aframax market, market participants continued to await a fresh test, with talk of significantly higher levels for north-bound voyages circulating in the market, although no firm details could be confirmed at the time of writing.
Strong short-haul daily earnings were giving owners confidence to seek substantially higher rates for long-haul voyages in order to achieve comparable daily returns, an Aframax broker said.
Oil companies with their own tonnage generally preferred to keep vessels for their own cargoes and favored short-haul employment, while independent owners were more inclined to position vessels for west-loading cargoes, a broker said. This left owners holding back on cargoes seeking vessels in Southeast Asia, said another Aframax broker.
LR2 tankers were also heard to be increasingly attracted to dirty trading due to stronger daily earnings, with a few LR2s heard to have switched to DPP employment, sources said.
In the Persian Gulf, Gulf of Oman-loading vessels were heard to have been taken on subjects, although no further details were available. On the fixture side, Vitol's Oct. 18 Fujairah-Colombo loading and its Oct. 20 Fujairah-Cilacap loading were both heard to have been covered.
-- Vickey Du

China resumes clean oil product exports after holiday halt; Oct volumes seen lower on month
Export restrictions lifted after holiday
October exports seen at 3.5 million mt
Jet fuel accounts for 60% of exports
China is moving ahead with clean oil product exports in October after state-owned refiners resumed export activities following a brief suspension over the Oct. 1-7 National Day holiday, although volumes are expected to fall from September levels, at least four domestic refinery sources told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, Oct. 9.
"Exports were allowed to resume late yesterday after a brief halt," a refinery source in South China said Oct. 9. The source previously told Platts Oct. 8 that no new export deals were permitted to be signed on the first working day after the holiday.
"There is no longer any restriction on export destinations," the refinery source added.

A separate refinery source in South China said a cargo bound for Vietnam was loaded during the holiday period, and export procedures could now proceed as normal.
"There have been constant changes regarding clean products exports," a Sinopec refinery source in East China said, while confirming that exports had resumed. The source added that the company's final approval for October export volumes had not yet been received.
A refinery source in North China said part of its refinery's export program had been reinstated.
"Jet fuel cargoes that had been canceled have recovered to around three cargoes as originally planned, but it is still unclear whether the canceled gasoline cargoes will be allowed to resume," the source said.
According to the source, one MR-sized gasoline cargo and two MR-sized jet fuel cargoes scheduled for October exports were earlier canceled Oct. 8, Platts reported earlier.

POWERR Asia, formally known as the Partnership on Wide Energy and Resources Resilience and launched in April by Japanese Prime Minister Sanae Takaichi, is a cooperation framework comprising both emergency and structural responses with mid- to long-term perspectives.
"I would like to convey our commitment to contributing to a more resilient global energy supply-demand structure by serving as a bridge between Middle Eastern oil-producing nations and Asian countries," Akazawa said.
Ministers from AZEC underscored their commitment to bolstering energy security and supply chain resilience, and to laying out a work program to facilitate the building of oil stockpiles amid disruptions in the Strait of Hormuz, according to a joint statement adopted Oct. 8.

Oct exports expected to decline Multiple analysts based in the country estimated China's
clean oil product exports at a minimum of 3.5 million metric tons in October, with jet fuel accounting for about 60% of the total volume.
They estimated October exports at around 2.1 million mt of jet fuel, 900,000 mt of gasoil, and 500,000 mt of gasoline.
The projected volume would be lower than September's estimated 4.36 million mt of clean products exports. China's customs data showed clean oil product exports totaled 4.58 million mt in August.
The anticipated month-over-month decline reflects lingering uncertainty over export approvals despite the resumption of export activity following the holiday period.

Japan to discuss AZEC oil stockpiling plan at IEF: minister
AZEC adopts work program for oil stockpiles
AZEC ministers endorse regional storage capacity boost
Japan intends to discuss the oil stockpiling plan presented at the fourth Asia Zero Emission Community Ministerial Meeting during the International Energy Forum ministerial meeting in Riyadh, Saudi Arabia, on Oct. 11, Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry Ryosei Akazawa said Oct. 9.
"This meeting, convened for the first time in eight years against the backdrop of the recent situation in the Middle East, will bring together oil-producing and oil-consuming countries," Akazawa told reporters in Tokyo. "It is being held at the initiative of Saudi energy minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman."
"From my side, I intend to discuss the policy direction for

Work program The ministers of the 11-nation AZEC partner countries of
Australia, Brunei, Cambodia, Indonesia, Japan, Laos, Malaysia, the Philippines, Singapore, Thailand and Vietnam also welcomed the adoption of the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling.
"Recognizing that each country will pursue various pathways, taking into account its national circumstances, we will advance efforts to strengthen domestic oil stockpiling capacity and enhance regional cooperation, particularly through the following steps, while utilizing POWERR Asia," according to the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document attached to the joint statement.
The adoption of the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling was a step forward in developing petroleum stockpiles among ASEAN countries, following years of little progress in establishing oil stockpile frameworks in the region.
Producer cooperation According to the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for
Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document, the first step toward strengthening the domestic oil stockpiling capacity is the establishment of appropriate stockpiling and release systems.
To expand the oil stockpiling capacity, the AZEC Ministerial Work Program for Strengthening Oil Stockpiling document noted that it is essential to cooperate with oil-producing countries, such as those in the Middle East, on a voluntary basis while respecting national sovereignty to build a more secure supply system.
Speaking at a joint press conference Oct. 8, following the AZEC Ministerial Meeting, Kei Takagi, Japan's state minister of economy, trade and industry, who co-chaired the meeting, said, "Saudi Arabia and the UAE were invited to participate in the first-ever ministerial-level session between Middle Eastern oilproducing countries and AZEC partner countries."

Hormuz traffic steady, oil flows rise despite heaviest ship attacks in months
October crossings hold well
Middle East crude exports match pre-war level
UKMTO records 18 attacks since Sept. 29

to Iran, the report said. After holding steady at 45 transits over the previous two days,
vessel movements at the Bab al-Mandab Strait fell to 32 on Oct. 8, the lowest daily level recorded since Sept. 16, CAS said. However, no Saudi-linked transits were observed, the report said. The Iran-backed, Yemen-based Houthi rebels have previously threatened Saudi ships in the waterway.

Traffic through the Strait of Hormuz has remained relatively steady so far in October as oil flows started to match pre-war levels, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, even as ship operators face the most intense attacks in months.
The choke point's ship crossings have averaged around 21.9 a day in the first eight days of this month, with 22 ships making the journey Oct. 8, according to CAS analysts. This compared with 23.7 transits in September.
Four VLCCs owned by Saudi Arabia's Bahri shipping company were among the 17 vessels that entered the Strait of Hormuz, the analysts said in an Oct. 9 report. Half of the ships that crossed the strait Oct. 8 were sanctioned or linked to Iran, according to the report.
Total Middle Eastern crude exports -- including those from the Red Sea, direct shipments via Hormuz, and loadings from Fujairah and via ship-to-ship transfers in the Gulf of Oman -- reached 18.2 million barrels per day on a seven-day average basis as of Oct. 3, according to a separate CAS report.
This came after total exports averaged 15.1 million b/d last month, including 10 million b/d originated from Persian Gulf ports. Exports between December and February averaged 17.1 million b/d.
Rising shipments came despite Iran launching its heaviest wave of attacks on merchant ships around Hormuz and beyond since the war began on Feb. 28.
The UK Maritime Trade Operations has recorded 18 ship attacks since Sept. 29, many of which have been identified by security consultancies as VLCCs.
"Last week, tanker attacks in the Middle East hit their highest levels since the war began and that pace has continued this week," Gibson Shipbrokers said in a note Oct. 9. "Further, in the past 48 hours, attacks have broadened to the Gulf of Oman and Arabian Gulf, expanding beyond the Hormuz chokepoint."
Ship attacks are continuing, as a vessel northwest of the UAE was attacked Oct. 9 by a projectile, the UKMTO said. Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps said it struck an LPG tanker south of the Strait of Hormuz that was traveling through what it called an "illegal" route.

South Korea investigates possible fuel shipments diversion to Russia
Seoul probes alleged fuel diversions to Russia
Diesel, jet fuel not on Korea's Russia export control list
Russia imports fuel amid refinery attacks
South Korea's government is investigating whether petroleum products loaded at its ports were diverted to Russia with their destination falsely declared, following a report that raised concerns about possible sanctions circumvention, although Seoul said the fuels involved were not subject to its export controls.
The refined petroleum products, such as commercial diesel and aviation fuel, did not fall under South Korean export control items to Russia, so their shipments from local ports did not violate domestic law, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs, the Ministry of Trade and the Customs Service said in a joint statement Oct. 8.
The statement was published in response to a report by The Guardian newspaper, which claimed Oct. 6 that fuel -- mostly diesel -- had been shipped from South Korea to Russia on sanctioned tankers. The sanctioned Astoria and Layla, which loaded fuel in South Korea's Ulsan, declared Japan as their destination, before sailing to Vladivostok, according to the report. The Chongchon took fuel from an unsanctioned tanker and declared Singapore as its destination before heading to Russia's Far East, the publication said.
The South Korean ministries stressed in a note that they had not confirmed whether the export of diesel and other South Korean fuels to Russia actually took place. They also cautioned that a vessel's deviation from a declared destination port did not by itself constitute conclusive evidence of a false declaration, noting that ships may transit or call at intermediate ports for

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

legitimate commercial reasons. According to Korea International Trade Association statistics
from 2025 through August 2026, diesel fuel, jet fuel, and gasoline were not exported from Korea to Russia, which means there were no cases of exports to Russia after customs clearance, the note said.
"Our government is reviewing additional information related to shipment cases reported by The Guardian, and if any violations of our laws are confirmed, we plan to take action in accordance with the law," the foreign affairs and trade ministries said.
Once a major refined products exporter, Russia became a motor fuel net importer this summer amid intensified Ukrainian attacks on the country's refineries. Almost half of Russia's primary refining capacity was offline at the end of September because of the strikes, according to S&P Global Energy CERA analysts.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, previously reported seaborne clean product shipments from South Korea to Russia, with S&P Global Commodities at Sea data capturing at least six cargoes on the route from the beginning of July, including four handysize vessels. The ship-tracking data did not allow for the establishment of the origin of the fuel on board.
South Korea is Asia's largest supplier and a net exporter of gasoline, gasoil and jet fuel, with established outlets and customer bases across the Asia-Pacific region. Therefore, there is no need for South Korean refiners to consider selling small parcels or spot cargoes to unfamiliar Russian entities, product managers at two refiners based in Ulsan told Platts Oct. 8.
In the first eight months of 2026, South Korea exported 62.03 million barrels of refined oil products, mostly diesel and gasoline, to its top customer Australia, up 18% from the same period a year earlier, according to the latest data from state-run Korea National Oil Corp. issued Sept. 25.
In addition to regular Asia-Pacific outlets, South Korea sold a record 5.73 million barrels of clean oil products, including gasoline, gasoil and jet fuel, to Europe in August, KNOC data showed.
Meanwhile, KNOC operates extensive commercial and strategic storage facilities in Ulsan and Yeosu under its Northeast Asia Oil Hub initiative. The facilities are designed to accommodate international trading companies, including major Western, Japanese and Chinese firms, the state-run oil and gas company said on its official website.
Consequently, not all tankers departing South Korean ports carry export shipments from South Korean refiners, the two product managers at Ulsan-based refiners said.
-- Alexandra Vladimirova, Gawoon Philip Vahn
UAE, Saudi Arabia sign MOU for rail and energy connectivity: WAM
Feasibility studies planned for cooperation
Deal comes as Hormuz crisis impacts ports and oil exports
The UAE and Saudi Arabia have signed a memorandum of understanding to work more closely on rail and energy

connectivity, UAE state-run news agency WAM reported Oct. 8. The countries, which have had some of their ports and oil
exports choked off by the crisis in the Strait of Hormuz, will work to develop rail connectivity between the countries and create infrastructure to improve energy-sector integration as part of the agreement, WAM said. The preliminary deal also includes cooperation on electricity interconnection, electricity exchange and sales, according to WAM.
The announcement provided limited details and WAM said feasibility studies will be carried out across the areas covered under the agreement.
The ongoing Strait of Hormuz crisis has triggered cross-border talks and new initiatives among Gulf Cooperation Council states and Middle Eastern countries as they work to mitigate the effects of shipping disruptions on their economies and energy trade.
Some work has been done on intra-GCC electricity sharing through the GCC Interconnection Authority, which was established in 2001, S&P Global Energy CERA said in a report in October 2025. By 2011, the six GCC nations were connected, but the grid was primarily used for emergency and reserve sharing only and cross-border flows were low, CERA said. More power has been traded for economic purposes, reaching 1.3 TWh in 2023, according to CERA.
-- Lauren Holtmeier

Along with crude oil, nearly 59% of natural gas production, or 1.26 billion cubic feet/day, is also shut-in, the MMA said in its 2 pm ET update. The latest curtailed volumes compare with 1.283 million boe/d and 1.13 Bcf/d as of Oct. 8.

Refinery updates
REFINERY NEWS: Libya's Agoco holds talks with Bluevee Energy for Tobruk, Sarir upgrades
Refinery: Tobruk, Libya
Owner: NOC subsidiary Arabian Gulf Oil Co.
Overall capacity: 20,000 barrels/day
Refinery: Sarir, Libya
Owner: Agoco
Overall capacity: 10,000 b/d

Arabian Gulf Oil Co., a subsidiary of Libya's National Oil Corp., has held talks with US energy infrastructure company Bluevee Energy aimed at upgrading the Tobruk and Sarir refineries.
In a statement posted on Facebook Oct. 8, Benghazi-based Agoco said the meeting between its top leadership and the deputy chairman of Bluevee "focused primarily on examining mechanisms and approaches for developing the Tobruk and Sarir refineries," as part of its "strategy to modernize the refining sector's infrastructure and improve its operational efficiency."
"The meeting forms part of ongoing, intensive efforts to strengthen the oil sector's production capabilities and contribute effectively to meeting the local market's basic needs for fuels and petroleum products," Agoco added.
Agoco said Bluevee specializes in engineering studies and manufacturing gas-processing and oil refinery equipment. Attempts by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, to reach Bluevee for comments did not yield a response Oct. 9.
The Sarir plant lies in southeast Libya and takes crude from Agoco's 200,000 barrels/day Sarir oil field. The Tobruk refinery is located in the eastern port city of Tobruk, close to the Egyptian border.
-- Elza Turner, Charlie Mitchell
REFINERY NEWS: Italy's Falconara remains operational after small fire
Refinery: Falconara Marittima, Ancona, Italy
Owner: IP (Italiana Petroli), owned by Socar
Overall capacity: 85,000 b/d
Italy's Falconara refinery remained operational after a small fire on Oct. 9, the company said in a statement Oct. 10.
"The ignition incident that occurred yesterday was promptly addressed and resolved by the refinery's internal response teams," it said, adding that the necessary inspections are being carried out in the affected area.
According to local media the fire occurred in the afternoon of Oct. 9 at a thermal cracking unit and was quickly contained by the refinery's own firefighters.
Azerbaijan's Socar said May 8 that it had concluded its buyout of Italiana Petroli, operator of Falconara and Sarpom refineries, from API Holding.
-- Elza Turner

larger offset to soaring jet-fuel costs in the fourth quarter after the plant returned to full operations following a temporary outage, helping the carrier mitigate the impacts of a $6 billion rise in annual fuel costs, company executives said on Oct. 9.
During its third-quarter results call, CFO Erik Snell said the refinery is fully back online after the temporary outage discussed in July, positioning the facility to provide a much larger hedge against elevated jet fuel prices.
The refinery reduced Delta's average fuel cost by $0.13/gal in the September quarter, and the airline expects the benefit to rise to about $0.40/gal in the December quarter--roughly three times the third-quarter level--while contributing more than $700 million for the full year, he said.
Even with the refinery offset, Delta projects an all-in fourthquarter fuel price of $4.25/gal based on the Oct. 2 forward curve. Fuel expense was $1.6 billion higher year over year in the third quarter--more than $500 million above the company's guidance--and management expects the full-year bill to climb 60%, or approximately $6 billion.
However, US Atlantic Coast refining margins are showing signs of weakening. As of Oct. 9, fourth-quarter-to-date USAC Bonny Light refinery cracking margins were averaging $12.13/b, trending 45% lower than Q3 2026 margin of $48.11/b, according to data from S&P Global.

crude oil and petroleum products assessments Oct. 12 due to the Thanksgiving holiday.
The Platts Market on Close assessment process for Americas oil Oct. 12 will remain unchanged, with a market close of 2:30 pm ET (1830 GMT).
Normal Canadian publishing schedules will resume Oct. 13. For full details on S&P Global Energy's publishing schedule and affected services, please refer to our holiday schedule. For inquiries, please contact pricegroup@spglobal.com.
Platts amends Abu Dhabi crude OSPs publication Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, has updated its publication
of Abu Dhabi crude official selling prices effective Oct. 8, 2026, in line with changes announced by Abu Dhabi National Oil Co.

©2026 S&P Global Inc. Any unauthorized use of this copyrighted material constitutes infringement of our intellectual property and will subject you to possible legal action.

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 9, 2026

(ADNOC). In July, ADNOC said it would adopt a prompt-month pricing
methodology based on the Platts Dubai benchmark (PCAAT00) plus an ADNOC-announced differential for the OSPs of its Abu Dhabi onshore and offshore crude grades, with effect from November 2026 loadings. ADNOC added that it would announce the OSP differentials for Murban, Das, Umm Lulu and Upper Zakum in the month prior to the delivery month.
Accordingly, Platts will publish the monthly OSP differentials for the four grades, basis Platts Dubai, beginning with the November differentials issued by ADNOC on Oct. 8, 2026.
Platts first announced the change in a subscriber note published on Aug. 31, 2026.
The OSP differentials are published under the following reinstated codes:

Grade Murban Umm Lulu Das Blend Upper Zakum

OSP Diff to Dubai AAIOS00 OSLUD00 AAXPL00 AAIOV00

Platts previously published the OSP differentials under these codes until May 2021, when they were then suspended due to a change in the ADNOC pricing methodology.
From November, the existing codes for the outright OSPs for Murban (AAIKZ00), Upper Zakum (AAILC00), Das Blend (AAXPH00) and Umm Lulu (OSLUF00) will remain unchanged. Platts will publish the outright OSPs of the four grades following the publication of the average of Platts Dubai crude assessments during the month of loading.
In view of the changes, Platts is no longer updating the codes reflecting Upper Zakum, Das Blend and Umm Lulu OSP differentials against Murban, as well as the codes for all four grades, including Murban, calculated against the monthly average of Platts cash Dubai 1st Line assessment in the month of trade.
The following codes will no longer be updated from October:

Grade Murban Umm Lulu Das Blend Upper Zakum

Diff to Dubai AOSPA00 AOSPB00 AOSPC00 AOSPD00

Diff to Murban OSP
AOSPE00 AOSPF00 AOSPG00

ADNOC's OSPs are published in the Platts price database and on Platts Global Alert page 1051.

Platts proposes amending freight calculation for Oman, Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments Nov. 2
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the freight calculation used in its daily Oman and Murban CFR North Asia crude assessments, effective Nov. 2, 2026.
Under the proposed change, Platts would use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (DOOEW00), which was launched Aug. 3, in its calculation of the outright price of Oman and Murban delivered into North Asia at 4:30 pm Singapore time (0830 GMT).

The proposed change would better reflect the shipping economics for the two grades, with Murban primarily loading from Fujairah port and Oman from Mina Al Fahal port outside the Strait of Hormuz.
Currently, the freight calculation is based on the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Far East freight assessment in $/mt (TDAAB00).
The freight calculation would continue to use the conversion factor for metric tons to barrels, as applied to the grades, in line with their prevailing qualities. The conversion factors are currently 7.32 for Oman and 7.62 for Murban.
In line with this change, Platts is also proposing to use the daily VLCC Gulf of Oman-to-Far East worldscale freight assessment (DIOEW00) in the freight calculation for Murban in its Indonesian Minas, Cinta and Widuri crude market parity prices.
Under the current MPP methodology for Minas, Cinta and Widuri, Murban -- as one of five basket grades -- is normalized for delivery to Singapore, accounting for freight. This is currently calculated using the daily VLCC Persian Gulf-to-Singapore worldscale freight assessment (PFAOD00).
The affected assessments appear in Crude Oil Marketwire, Oilgram Price Report, LNG Daily, Platts OPR World Crude Table, Platts Global Alert/Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 1250, 1805, 2202, 2203, 2211, 2216, 2217, 2244, 2245, 2264, 2265, 2280, 2281, 2811 and 2812, LNG Alert pages 0050 and 0051, Platts Natural Gas Alert pages 1024 and 1069, and under the price database codes below:

Assessments Oman CFR North Asia Murban CFR North Asia Minas Singapore close Minas London close Cinta Singapore close Cinta London close Widuri Singapore close Widuri London close Minas FOB Indonesia $/

The new assessments will complement Platts' existing daily and weekly bitumen assessments, providing greater price transparency and information in the waterborne trade for the product in the Asia-Pacific and Middle East markets.

Platts proposes to amend rollover date of Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, is proposing to amend the rollover date of its Asian Dated Brent Strip at London close assessment to the first publication day of the month, from Dec. 1, 2026.
The proposed change, which follows Platts review of historical data sets, would align the ADB Strip at London close assessment with the current rollover date of its ADB Strip at Asia close assessment.
Currently, this assessment, which reflects the forward value of Dated Brent two months ahead of the date of publication at the

4:30 pm London close, rolls over on the 9th of the month. The assessments affected by this change appear in Crude
Oil Marketwire, Asia Pacific/Arab Gulf Marketscan, Oilgram Price Report, Platts Global Alert/ Platts Crude Oil Alert pages 2206, 2213, 2215, 2217, 2219, 2260, 2261, 2262, 2263, 2264, 2265, 2266, 2267, 2268, 2269, 2270, 2271, 2280, 2281, 2558, 2559, and under their price database codes as below:

Fossil fuels dominate the energy mix representing over 80% of global energy demand.
